{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Q_{1}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus\cdots\oplus\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Q_{n}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
wherever possible, which both cover all the outcomes (for correctness) and also guarantee reachability of each
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{k}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
(for bug finding). Many procedures—even looping ones—fit into this format,
e.g.,
the one we saw in
Section

7.1
.

On the other hand, some procedures will need to be analyzed using specialized techniques. For example, the use of a loop invariant in
Section

7.2
introduced a
{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}
modality to indicate that there may not be any terminating outcomes. Alternatively, if a bug arises in one of the paths, the analysis can introduce a
{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Diamond}
modality in order to retain less information about the other paths. Both of these can be achieved by an application of the rule of consequence.

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Q_{1}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus\cdots\oplus\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Q_{n}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\quad\forall k.\ Q_{k}\Rightarrow Q}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}Q{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\qquad\qquad\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Q_{1}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus\cdots\oplus\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Q_{n}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Diamond}Q_{k}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}

Zilberstein
et al. 2024a
designed an algorithm for this type of analysis, showing not only that Outcome Logic models Infer and Pulse, but also that the engines of those tools could be consolidated so as to share procedure specifications in many cases. That algorithm is described in
Section

10
.

8.
Case Study: Graph Problems

We now examine case studies using Outcome Logic to derive quantitative properties in alternative models of computation.

8.1.
Counting Random Walks

Suppose we wish to count the number of paths between the origin and the point
(N,M)
on a two dimensional grid. To achieve this, we first write a program that performs a random walk on the grid; while the destination is not yet reached, it nondeterministically chooses to take a step on either the
x
or
y
-axis (or steps in a fixed direction if the destination on one axis is already reached).

\texttt{Walk}\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{while}~x<N\vee y<M~\texttt{do}\\
\quad\texttt{if}~x<N\land y<M~\texttt{then}\\
\quad\quad(x\coloneqq x+1)+(y\coloneqq y+1)\\
\quad\texttt{else if}~x\geq N~\texttt{then}\\
\quad\quad y\coloneqq y+1\\
\quad\texttt{else}\\
\quad\quad x\coloneqq x+1\end{array}\right.

Using a standard program logic, it is relatively easy to prove that the program will always terminate in a state where
x=N
and
y=M
. However, we are going to interpret this program using the
\mathsf{Nat}
semiring (
Example

3.9
) in order to count how many traces (
i.e.,
random walks) reach that outcome.

First of all, we know it will take exactly
N+M
steps to reach the destination, so we can analyze the program using the
Variant
rule, where the loop variant
\varphi_{n}
records the state of the program
n
steps away from reaching
(N,M)
.

If we are
n
steps away, then there are several outcomes ranging from
x=N-n\land y=M
to
x=N\land y=M-n
. More precisely, let
k
be the distance to
N
on the
x
-axis, meaning that the distance to
M
on the
y
-axis must be
n-k
, so
x=N-k
and
y=M-(n-k)
. At all times, it must be true that
0\leq x\leq N
and
0\leq y\leq M
, so it must also be true that
0\leq N-k\leq N
and
0\leq M-(n-k)\leq M
. solving for
k
, we get that
0\leq k\leq N
and
n-M\leq k\leq n
. So,
k
can range between
\max(0,n-M)
and
\min(N,n)
.

In addition, the number of paths to
(x,y)
is
{x+y\choose x}
,
i.e.,
the number of ways to pick
x
steps on the
x
-axis out of
x+y
total steps. Putting all of that together, we define our loop variant as follows:

\varphi_{n}\triangleq\bigoplus_{k=\max(0,n-M)}^{\min(N,n)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=N-k\land y=M-(n-k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-n\choose N-k})}

The loop body moves the program state from
\varphi_{n+1}
to
\varphi_{n}
. The outcomes of
\varphi_{n+1}
get divided among the three if branches. In the outcome where
x=N
already,
y
must step, so this goes to the second branch. Similarly, if
y=M
already, then
x
must step, corresponding to the third branch. All other outcomes go to the first branch, which further splits into two outcomes due to the nondeterministic choice.
Since we start
N+M
steps from the destination, we get the following precondition:

\varphi_{N+M}=\bigoplus_{k=N}^{N}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=N-k\land y=N-k\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({0\choose N-N})}=\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=0\land y=0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil

In addition, the postcondition is:

\varphi_{0}=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{0}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=N-k\land y=M+k\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M\choose N})}=\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=N\land y=M\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M\choose N})}

This gives us the final specification below, which tells us that there are
{N+M\choose N}
paths to reach
(N,M)
from the origin. The full derivation is given in
Section

H.1
.

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=0\land y=0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{Walk}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=N\land y=M\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M\choose N})}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

8.2.
Shortest Paths

We will now use an alternative interpretation of computation to analyze a program that nondeterministically finds the shortest path from
s
to
t
in a directed graph.
Let
G
be the
N\times N
Boolean adjacency matrix of a directed graph, so that
G[i][j]=\mathsf{true}
if there is an edge from
i
to
j
(or
\mathsf{false}
if no such edge exists). We also add the following expression syntax to read edge weights in a program, noting that
G[E_{1}][E_{2}]\in\mathsf{Test}
since it is Boolean-valued.

E::=\cdots\mid G[E_{1}][E_{2}]

\left\llbracket G[E_{1}][E_{2}]\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Exp}}(s)\triangleq G\left[\left\llbracket E_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Exp}}(s)\right]\left[\left\llbracket E_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Exp}}(s)\right]

The following program loops until the current position
pos
reaches the destination
t
. At each step, it nondeterministically chooses which edge (
next)
to traverse using an iterator; for all
next\leq N
, each trace is selected if there is an edge from
pos
to
next
, and a weight of 1 is then added to the path, signifying that we took a step.

\texttt{SP}\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{while}~pos\neq t~\texttt{do}\\
\quad next\coloneqq 1\fatsemi\\
\quad{\left(next\coloneqq next+1\right)}^{\langle next<N,~~G[pos][next]\rangle}\fatsemi\\
\quad pos\coloneqq next\fatsemi\\
\quad\texttt{assume}~1\end{array}\right.

We will interpret this program using the
\mathsf{Tropical}
semiring from
Example

3.11
, in which addition corresponds to
\min
and multiplication corresponds to addition. So, path lengths get accumulated via addition and nondeterministic choices correspond to taking the path with minimal weight. That means that at the end of the program execution, we should end up in a scenario where
pos=t
, with weight equal to the shortest path length from
s
to
t
.

To prove this, we first formalize the notion of shortest paths below:
\mathsf{sp}^{t}_{n}(G,s,s^{\prime})
indicates whether there is a path of length
n
from
s
to
s^{\prime}
in
G
in without passing through
t
and
\mathsf{sp}(G,s,t)
is the shortest path length from
s
to
t
. Let
I=\{1,\ldots,N\}\setminus\{t\}
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{sp}_{0}^{t}(G,s,s^{\prime})

\displaystyle\triangleq(s=s^{\prime})

\displaystyle\mathsf{sp}_{n+1}^{t}(G,s,s^{\prime})

\displaystyle\triangleq\bigvee_{i\in I}\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,i)\land G[i][s^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\mathsf{sp}(G,s,t)

\displaystyle\triangleq{\inf{\{n\in\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\mid\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,t)\}}}

We analyze the while loop using the
While
rule, which requires
\varphi_{n}
and
\psi_{n}
to record the outcomes where the loop guard is true or false, respectively, after
n
iterations. We define these as follows, where
+
denotes regular arithmetic addition rather than addition in the tropical semiring.

\varphi_{n}=\bigoplus_{i\in I}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=i\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,i)+n)}\qquad\psi_{n}=\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}pos=t\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,t)+n)}\qquad\psi_{\infty}=\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}pos=t\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\mathsf{sp}(G,s,t))}

Recall that in the tropical semiring
\mathsf{false}=\infty
and
\mathsf{true}=0
. So, after
n
iterations, the weight of the outcome
pos=i
is equal to
n
if there is an
n
-step path from
s
to
i
, and
\infty
otherwise. The final postcondition
\psi_{\infty}
is the shortest path length to
t
, which is also the infimum of
\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,t)+n
for all
n
. Using the
Iter
rule we get the following derivation for the inner loop:

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bigoplus_{i\in I}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=i\land next=1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,i)+n)}}\right\rangle}\\
\quad{{\left(next\coloneqq next+1\right)}^{\langle next<N,~~G[pos][next]\rangle}}\fatsemi\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bigoplus_{j=1}^{N}{\bigoplus_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=i\land next=j\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{((\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,i)\land G[i][j])+n)}}\right\rangle}\\
\quad pos\coloneqq next\fatsemi\texttt{assume}~1\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bigoplus_{j=1}^{N}\bigoplus_{i\in I}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=j\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{((\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,i)\land G[i][j])+n+1)}}\right\rangle}\implies\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bigoplus_{j=1}^{N}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=j\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\mathsf{sp}_{n+1}^{t}(G,s,j)+n+1)}}\right\rangle}\\
\end{array}

The outcome conjunction over
i\neq t
(corresponding to the minimum weight path) gives us
pos=j
with weight
\mathsf{sp}_{n+1}^{t}(G,s,j)+n+1
—it is
n+1
if there is path of length
n
to some
i
and
G[i][j]
.

The precondition is
\varphi_{0}\oplus\psi_{0}=(pos=s)
, since
\mathsf{sp}_{0}^{t}(G,s,i)=\mathsf{false}
when
i\neq s
and
\mathsf{true}
when
i=s
.
Putting this all together, we get the following triple, stating that the final position is
t
and the weight is equal to the shortest path.

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=s\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{SP}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}pos=t\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\mathsf{sp}(G,s,t))}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

This program does not terminate if there is no path from
s
to
t
. In that case there are no reachable outcomes, so the interpretation of the program is
\dsser{0}
. Indeed,
\dsser{0}=\infty
in the tropical semiring, which is the shortest path between two disconnected nodes. The postcondition is
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=t\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\infty)}
, meaning that the program diverged.

9.
Related Work

Correctness, Incorrectness, and Unified Program Logics.

While formal verification has long been the aspiration for static analysis, bug-finding tools are often more practical in real-world engineering settings. This partly comes down to efficiency—bugs can be found without considering all traces—and partly due to the fact that most real world software just is not correct
(
Distefano et al. 2019
)
.

However, the standard logical foundations of program analysis such as Hoare Logic are prone to
false positives
when used for bug-finding—they cannot witness the existence of erroneous traces. In response,
O’Hearn 2020
developed Incorrectness Logic, which under-approximates the reachable states (as opposed to Hoare Logic’s over-approximation) so as to only report bugs that truly occur.

Although Incorrectness Logic successfully serves as a logical foundation for bug-finding tools
(
Raad et al. 2020
;
Le et al. 2022
)
, it is semantically incompatible with correctness analysis, making sharing of toolchains difficult or impossible. Attention has therefore turned to unifying correctness and incorrectness theories. This includes Exact Separation Logic, which combines Hoare Logic and Incorrectness Logic to generate specifications that are valid for both, but that also precludes under- or over-approximation via the rule of consequence
(
Maksimović et al. 2023
)
. Local Completeness Logic combines Incorrectness Logic with an over-approximate abstract domain to similar effect; it also precludes dropping paths
(
Bruni
et al. 2021
;
Bruni
et al. 2023
)
.

There have also been recent efforts to organize correctness and incorrectness logics into taxonomies, where their similarities and differences are expressed in terms of weakening, contrapositives, and Galois connections
(
Cousot 2024
;
Zhang and
Kaminski 2022
;
Ascari
et al. 2025
;
Verscht and
Kaminski 2025
)
.
Our goal in this article is orthogonal; we capture many different types of specifications with a
single
semantics and proof theory, so as to reuse proof fragments as much as possible. We also include several kinds of effects rather than just nondeterminism.

Outcome Logic.

Outcome Logic unifies correctness and incorrectness reasoning without compromising the use of logical consequences. This builds on an idea colloquially known as
Lisbon Logic
, first proposed by Derek Dreyer and Ralf Jung in 2019, that has similarities to the diamond modality of Dynamic Logic
(
Pratt 1976
;
Harel
et al. 2001
)
and
Hoare 1978
’s [
(
Hoare 1978
)
] calculus of
possible correctness
. The idea was briefly mentioned in the Incorrectness Logic literature
(
O’Hearn 2020
;
Möller
et al. 2021
;
Le et al. 2022
)
, but using Lisbon Logic as a foundation of incorrectness analysis was not fully explored until the introduction of Outcome Logic
(
Zilberstein
et al. 2023
)
, which subsumes both Lisbon Logic and Hoare Logic, as we saw in
Section

6.1
. The metatheory of Lisbon Logic has subsequently been explored more deeply and extended
(
Ascari
et al. 2025
;
Raad
et al. 2024
)
. Hyper Hoare Logic also generalizes Hoare and Lisbon Logics
(
Dardinier and
Müller 2024
)
, and is semantically equivalent to the Boolean instance of OL (
Example

3.7
), but does not support effects other than nondeterminism.

Outcome Logic initially used a model based on both a monad and a monoid, with looping defined via the Kleene star
C^{\star}

(
Zilberstein
et al. 2023
)
. The semantics of
C^{\star}
had to be justified for each instance,
however it is not compatible with probabilistic computation (see
Footnote

2
), so an ad-hoc semantics was used in the probabilistic case.
Moreover, only the
Induction
rule was provided for reasoning about
C^{\star}
, amounting to unrolling the loop one time. Some loops can be analyzed by applying
Induction
repeatedly, but it is inadequate if the number of iterations depends at all on the program state.
Our
{C}^{\langle e,~e^{\prime}\rangle}
construct fixes this, defining iteration in a way that supports both Kleene star (
{C}^{\langle\dsser{1},~\dsser{1}\rangle}
) and also probabilistic computation. As we showed in
Sections

7
and
8
,
Iter
can be used to reason about any loop, even ones that iterate an unbounded number of times.

The next Outcome Logic paper focused on a particular separation logic instance
(
Zilberstein
et al. 2024a
)
. The model was refined to use semirings, and the programming language included while loops instead of
C^{\star}
so that a single well-definedness proof could extend to all instances. However, the evaluation model included additional constraints (
\dsser{1}=\top
and normalization) that preclude,
e.g.,
the multiset model (
Example

3.9
). Rather than giving inference rules, the paper provided a symbolic execution algorithm, which also only supported loops via bounded unrolling.

This article extends the prior conference papers on Outcome Logic by giving a more general model with more instances and better support for iteration, providing a relatively complete proof system that is able to handle any loop, deriving additional inference rules for Hoare and Lisbon Logic embeddings, and exploring case studies related to previously unsupported types of computation and looping.

Computational Effects.

Effects have been present since the early years of program analysis. Even basic programming languages with while loops introduce the possibility of
nontermination
. Partial correctness was initially used to sidestep the termination question
(
Floyd 1967a
;
Hoare 1969
)
, but total correctness (requiring termination) was later introduced too
(
Manna and Pnueli 1974
)
. More recently, automated tools were developed to prove (non)termination in real-world software
(
Berdine
et al. 2006
;
Brockschmidt
et al. 2013
;
Cook
et al. 2014
;
Cook
et al. 2006a
;
Cook
et al. 2006b
;
Raad
et al. 2024
)
.

Nondeterminism also showed up in early variants of Hoare Logic, stemming from Dijkstra’s Guarded Command Language (GCL)
(
Dijkstra 1975
)
and Dynamic Logic
(
Pratt 1976
;
Harel
et al. 2001
)
;
it is useful for modeling backtracking algorithms
(
Floyd 1967b
)
and opaque aspects of program evaluation such as user input and concurrent scheduling. While Hoare Logic has traditionally used demonic nondeterminism
(
Broy and Wirsing 1981
)
, other program logics have recently arisen to deal with nondeterminism in different ways, particularly for incorrectness
(
de Vries and
Koutavas 2011
;
O’Hearn 2020
;
Möller
et al. 2021
;
Zilberstein
et al. 2023
;
Raad
et al. 2024
;
Ascari
et al. 2025
;
Dardinier and
Müller 2024
)
.

Beginning with the seminal work of
Kozen 1979
;
Kozen 1983
, the study of probabilistic programs has a rich history. This eventually led to the development of probabilistic Hoare Logic variants
(
den Hartog 2002
;
Barthe et al. 2018
;
Rand and
Zdancewic 2015
;
den Hartog 1999
)
that enable reasoning about programs in terms of likelihoods and expected values.
Doing so requires pre- and postconditions to be predicates on probability distributions.

Outcome Logic provides abilities to reason about those effects using a common set of inference rules. This opens up the possibility for static analysis tools that soundly share proof fragments between different types of programs, as shown in
Section

7
.

Relative Completeness and Expressivity.

Any sufficiently expressive program logic must necessarily be incomplete since, for example, the Hoare triple
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\{}}\mathsf{true}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\}}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\{}}\mathsf{false}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\}}}
states that the program
C
never halts, which is not provable in an axiomatic deduction system. In response,
Cook 1978
devised the idea of
relative
completeness to convey that a proof system is adequate for analyzing a program, but not necessarily assertions about the program states.

Expressivity requires that the assertion language used in pre- and postconditions can describe the intermediate program states needed to,
e.g.,
apply the
Seq
rule. In other words, the assertion syntax must be able to express
\mathsf{post}(C,P)
from
Definition

4.5
. Implications for an
expressive
language quickly become undecidable, as they must encode Peano arithmetic
(
Apt 1981
;
Lipton 1977
)
.
With this in mind, the best we can hope for is a program logic that is complete
relative
to an oracle that decides implications in the rule of
Consequence
.

The question of what an expressive (syntactic) assertion language for Outcome Logic looks like remains open. In fact, the question of expressive assertion languages for probabilistic Hoare Logics (which are subsumed by Outcome Logic) is also open
(
den Hartog 1999
;
Barthe et al. 2018
)
.
den Hartog 2002
devised a relatively complete probabilistic logic with syntactic assertions, but the programming language does not include loops and is therefore considerably simplified; it is unclear if this approach would extend to looping programs. In addition
Batz
et al. 2021
created an expressive language for expectation-based reasoning, however the language only has constructs to describe states and expected values; it does not contain a construct like
\oplus
to express properties involving of multiple traces of the program. As we saw in the discussion following
Definition

4.5
, the ability to describe multiple executions makes the expressivity question more complex.

Several program logics (including our own) use semantic assertions, which are trivially expressive
(
Calcagno
et al. 2007
;
Yang 2001
;
O’Hearn 2020
;
Jung et al. 2015
;
Jung et al. 2018
;
Barthe et al. 2018
;
Kaminski 2019
;
Dardinier and
Müller 2024
;
Raad
et al. 2024
;
Cousot
et al. 2012
;
Ascari
et al. 2025
)
. This includes logics that are mechanized within proof assistants
(
Barthe et al. 2018
;
Jung et al. 2018
;
Jung et al. 2015
;
Dardinier and
Müller 2024
)
, so we do not see the extensional nature of our approach as a barrier to mechanization in the future.

Quantitative Reasoning and Weighted Programming.

Whereas Hoare Logic provides a foundation for
propositional
program analysis, quantitative program analysis has been explored too.
Probabilistic Propositional Dynamic Logic
(
Kozen 1983
)
and weakest pre-expectation calculi
(
Morgan
et al. 1996a
;
Kaminski 2019
;
McIver and Morgan 2005
)
are used to reason about randomized programs in terms of expected values. This idea has been extended to non-probabilistic quantitative properties too
(
Zhang and
Kaminski 2022
;
Batz et al. 2022
;
Zhang
et al. 2024
)
.

Weighted programming
(
Batz et al. 2022
)
generalizes pre-expectation reasoning using semirings to model branch weights, much like this paper. Outcome Logic is a propositional analogue to weighted programming’s quantitative model, but it is also more expressive in its ability to reason about quantities over
multiple outcomes
. For example, in
Section

7.3
, we derive a single OL triple that gives the probabilities of two outcomes, whereas weighted programming (or weakest pre-expectations) would need to compute the weight of each branch individually. In addition,
Batz et al. 2022
only support total semirings, so they cannot analyze standard probabilistic programs. Weighted programming was extended to handle hyperproperties by
Zhang
et al. 2024
, which can be seen as a weakest precondition calculus for Outcome Logic.

10.
Conclusion, Limitations, and Future Work

In this article, we have presented a proof system for Outcome Logic, which is sufficient for reasoning about programs with branching effects. That is, effects that can be semantically encoded by assigning weights to a collection of final states. However, there remains room for future development of Outcome Logic in order to support additional kinds of effects and other capabilities. In this section, we describe the limitations of the present formalization, and how those limitations could be addressed in future work.

Total Correctness and Nontermination.

Although Outcome Logic provides some ability to reason about nontermination—
i.e.,
it can be used to prove that a program
sometimes
or
never
terminates—it cannot be used for total correctness
(
Manna and Pnueli 1974
)
, as the semantics only tracks terminating traces, not the existence of infinite ones. Given some specification
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
, it is therefore impossible to know whether additional nonterminating behavior outside of
\psi
is possible.

The challenge in tracking infinite traces is that the semantics can easily become non-continuous, making it more difficult to prove the existence of fixed points used to define the semantics of loops. More precisely,
Apt and Plotkin 1986
showed that no continuous semantics is possible that both distinguishes nontermination and also supports unbounded nondeterminism. That means that, at least in the nondeterministic interpretation (
Example

3.7
), a primitive action of the following form, which assigns
x
to a nondeterministically chosen natural number, must be precluded.

\left\llbracket x\coloneqq\bigstar\right\rrbracket(s)\quad\triangleq\quad\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\eta(s[x\coloneqq n])\quad\cong\quad\{s[x\coloneqq n]\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}

Note that in the present formulation, we could define the above construct as syntactic sugar, as we did in
Section

6.2
. The program below has the exact same semantics as above, since our model in
Section

3
does not record the fact that it also has an infinite (nonterminating) execution.

x\coloneqq\bigstar\quad\triangleq\quad x\coloneqq 0\fatsemi(x\coloneqq x+1)^{\star}

However, in a total correctness version, the semantics of those programs would not be the same; the version defined with the Kleene star has an additional nonterminating trace.
This corresponds to
Dijkstra 1976
’s [
(
Dijkstra 1976
)
] observation that a program cannot make infinitely many choices in a finite amount of time, giving an operational argument for why unbounded nondeterminism should not be allowed. However, the story is more murky when generalizing to the semiring weighted model that we have presented in this paper. Although unbounded choice is not valid in the nondeterministic model, it
is
valid in the probabilistic model (
Example

3.10
). For example, the following probabilistic program makes infinitely many choices, but it also
almost surely terminates
(there is an infinite trace, but it occurs with probability 0, so we do not consider it a possible outcome).

x\coloneqq 0\fatsemi(x\coloneqq x+1)^{\langle\frac{1}{2}\rangle}

So, in the total correctness setting, the continuity of program operators depends on the semiring in a non-obvious way, which cannot be explained by existing work on powerdomains
(
Plotkin 1976
;
Smyth 1978
;
Søndergaard and
Sestoft 1992
)
. Some work in this area has already been done by
Li
et al. 2024
, who develop a semantic model for Total Outcome Logic that explains this discrepancy in terms of properties of the semirings.

Mixing Effects.

While Outcome Logic, as described in this article, can be used to analyze programs that have various types of branching, it cannot handle programs that display multiple kinds of branching in tandem. Of particular interest is the combination of probabilistic choice and nondeterminism, which would be useful to analyze randomized distributed systems (where nondeterminism arises due to concurrent scheduling).
Unfortunately, the typical powerset monad representation of nondeterminism (
Example

3.7
) does not compose well with probability distributions (
Example

3.10
)
(
Varacca and
Winskel 2006
)
, and similar restrictions apply to other combinations of semirings
(
Parlant 2020
;
Zwart and Marsden 2019
;
Zwart 2020
)
.

Demonic Outcome Logic (DOL)
(
Zilberstein et al. 2025b
)
was recently introduced for reasoning about programs that are both probabilistic and nondeterministic, with semantics based on the convex powerset monad
(
Morgan
et al. 1996b
;
He
et al. 1997
)
, a specialized computational domain, which does not generalize like the semiring model that we use in this paper does. DOL supports reasoning about
probabilistic
outcomes with
\oplus
, whereas nondeterminism is treated demonically (the postcondition applies to all nondeterministic paths). This makes DOL a correctness logic only, it cannot witness the
existence
of bad distributions of outcomes. It would be interesting to develop a dual
Angelic Outcome Logic
for reasoning about incorrectness, or even a logic that unifies the two.

Alternatively, it was recently shown that probabilistic monads do compose with multisets
(
Jacobs 2021
;
Kozen and Silva 2023
)
. In
Section

8.1
, we showed how the Outcome Logic instance based on multisets (
Example

3.9
) can be used for quantitative analysis, however that example did not correspond to a canonical model of computation. By contrast, a logic using that same multiset instance for probabilistic nondeterminism would model a very pertinent combination of effects, motivating the study of these more exotic forms of nondeterminism in this paper. The powerdomain of indexed valuations
(
Varacca 2002
;
Varacca and
Winskel 2006
)
is a similar computational domain that is also constructed via a distributive law between two weighting functions. Given these many different models, it would be interesting to investigate a variant of Outcome Logic where multiple weighting functions can be composed together.

Mutable State, Separation Logic, and Concurrency.

As an extension to our Outcome Logic formulation in this article, it would be possible to add atomic commands for dynamically allocating and mutating heap pointers, and separation logic
(
Reynolds 2002
;
O’Hearn
et al. 2001
)
style inference rules to reason about mutation. Separation logic introduces new logical primitives such as the points-to predicate
x\mapsto E
—stating that the
x
is a pointer whose address maps to the value of
E
on the heap—and the separating conjunction
P\ast Q
—stating that
P
and
Q
hold in disjoint regions of the heap, guaranteeing that if
x\mapsto E_{1}\ast y\mapsto E_{2}
holds, then
x
and
y
do not alias each other.

This idea was already explored in Outcome Separation Logic (OSL)
(
Zilberstein
et al. 2024a
)
, but questions relating to unbounded looping and completeness were not addressed in that work. In separation logic, it is typical to provide
small axioms

(
O’Hearn
et al. 2001
)
, which specify the behavior of mutation locally. For example, the following
Write
rule specifies the behavior of mutation in a singleton heap. Global reasoning is then done via the
Frame
rule
(
Yang and O’Hearn 2002
)
, which states that a proof done in a local footprint is also valid in a larger heap.

\hypertarget{rule:Write}{}\frac{\;}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\{}}x\mapsto-{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\}}}~[x]\leftarrow E~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\{}}x\mapsto E{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\}}}}{\textsc{Write}}\qquad\qquad\hypertarget{rule:Frame}{}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\{}}P{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\}}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\{}}Q{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\{}}P\ast F{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\}}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\{}}Q\ast F{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\}}}}{\textsc{Frame}}

Yang 2001
showed that the small axioms along with the
Frame
rule are complete for standard separation logic; any specification can be derived using them. By contrast,
Zilberstein
et al. 2024a
showed that the small axioms and
Frame
rule are sound in OSL, but it is not known whether they are complete. In fact, there is some evidence to the contrary, since the resources specified outside the footprint of the local mutation may take on many different values, and therefore the
Frame
rule would need to be applied for each branch of the computation. OSL could be obtained as an instantiation of the Outcome Logic model in this article, although the
Frame
rule would need to be added (and as such, this article does not fully subsume OSL). It would be interesting to add such a frame rule, and explore the notion of completeness in that setting.

Zilberstein
et al. 2024a
also developed an algorithm to mechanically derive triples of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Q_{1}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus\cdots\oplus\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Q_{n}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
, using the
Seq
and
Choice
rules to compositionally reason about sequential compositions via the following derived rule.

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bigoplus_{i\in I}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Q_{i}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\quad\forall i\in I.\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}Q_{i}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bigoplus_{j\in J_{i}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}R_{j}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bigoplus_{i\in I,j\in J_{i}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}R_{j}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}

The
Frame
rule is a crucial part of the algorithm too; it is used to lift axioms about actions—such as the
Write
rule above—into the current program context. The particular frames are inferred using
biabduction

(
Calcagno et al. 2009
)
, a heuristic procedure for inferring the
missing
antiframe
M
and leftover
frame

F
in the following entailment
P\ast M\vdash Q\ast F
. So, for any precondition
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
, a write action can be analyzed as follows:

\frac{P\ast M\vdash(x\mapsto-)\ast F\quad\frac{\frac{\;}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x\mapsto-\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~[x]\leftarrow E~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x\mapsto E\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Write}{{Write}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(x\mapsto-)\ast F\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~[x]\leftarrow E~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}(x\mapsto E)\ast F\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Frame}{{Frame}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\ast M\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~[x]\leftarrow E~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}(x\mapsto E)\ast F\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{{Consequence}}}

One weakness of the algorithm is that it only has the ability to reason about loops via bounded unrolling. It would be interesting to extend the algorithm to use some of loop rules that we presented in this article. In particular, an algorithm could be developed for approximating loop behavior in Lisbon Logic using abstract domains
(
Cousot and Cousot 1977
)
, something that cannot be done in Incorrectness Logic
(
Ascari
et al. 2022
)
.

Going further, it would also be interesting to explore a concurrent variant of OSL. Along with the aforementioned challenges of reasoning about weighted branching in tandem with the nondeterministic behavior of the concurrent scheduler, this would also require a more powerful version of separation than that offered by OSL. The OSL separating conjunction
\varphi\circledast P
is asymmetric;
\varphi
is an unrestricted outcome assertion, whereas
P
can only be a basic assertion, like those described in
Section

4.1
. However, concurrent separation logic
(
O’Hearn 2004
)
typically requires the entire state space to be separated in order to compositionally analyze concurrent threads, so a symmetric separating conjunction
\varphi\circledast\psi
would be needed. Preliminary work has already been done to this effect
(
Zilberstein et al. 2024b
;
Zilberstein
et al. 2025a
)
based on Probabilistic Separation Logic
(
Barthe
et al. 2019
)
.

Computational effects have traditionally beckoned disjoint program logics across two dimensions: different
kinds
of effects (
e.g.,
nondeterminism vs randomization) and different
assertions
about those effects (
e.g.,
angelic vs demonic nondeterminism).
Outcome Logic
(
Zilberstein
et al. 2023
;
Zilberstein
et al. 2024a
)
captures those properties in a unified way, but until now the proof theory and connections to other logics have not been thoroughly explored.

This article expands on the prior Outcome Logic work and provides a comprehensive account of the OL metatheory by presenting a relatively complete proof system for Outcome Logic and a significant number of derived rules.
This shows that programs with branching effects are not only
semantically
similar, but also share common reasoning principles. Specialized techniques (
i.e.,
analyzing loops with variants or invariants) are particular modes of use of our more general logic, and are compatible with each other rather than requiring distinct semantics.

Appendix

Appendix A
Totality of Language Semantics

A.1.
Semantics of Tests and Expressions

Given some semiring
\langle U,+,\cdot,\dsser{0},\dsser{1}\rangle
, the definition of the semantics of tests
\left\llbracket b\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Test}}\colon\Sigma\to\{\dsser{0},\dsser{1}\}
is below.

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\mathsf{true}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Test}}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\triangleq\dsser{1}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\mathsf{false}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Test}}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\triangleq\dsser{0}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket b_{1}\vee b_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Test}}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\dsser{1}&\text{if}~\left\llbracket b_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Test}}(\sigma)=\dsser{1}~\text{or}~\left\llbracket b_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Test}}(\sigma)=\dsser{1}\\
\dsser{0}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\left\llbracket b_{1}\land b_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Test}}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\dsser{1}&\text{if}~\left\llbracket b_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Test}}(\sigma)=\dsser{1}~\text{and}~\left\llbracket b_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Test}}(\sigma)=\dsser{1}\\
\dsser{0}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\lnot b\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Test}}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\dsser{1}&\text{if}~\left\llbracket b\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Test}}(\sigma)=\dsser{0}\\
\dsser{0}&\text{if}~\left\llbracket b\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Test}}(\sigma)=\dsser{1}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Test}}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\dsser{1}&\text{if}~\sigma\in t\\
\dsser{0}&\text{if}~\sigma\notin t\end{array}\right.

Based on that, we define the semantics of expressions
\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket\colon\Sigma\to U
.

\displaystyle\left\llbracket b\right\rrbracket(\sigma)

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\llbracket b\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Test}}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\left\llbracket u\right\rrbracket(\sigma)

\displaystyle\triangleq u

A.2.
Fixed Point Existence

For all the proofs in this section, we assume that the operations
+
,
\cdot
, and
\sum
belong to a Scott continuous, naturally ordered, partial semiring with a top element (as described in
Section

3.3
).

Lemma A.1.

Let
\langle U,+,\cdot,\dsser{0},\dsser{1}\rangle
be a continuous, naturally ordered, partial semiring. For any family of Scott continuous functions
(f_{i}:X\to\mathcal{W}(Y))_{i\in I}
and directed set
D\subseteq X
:

\sup_{x\in D}\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}(x)=\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}(\sup D)

Proof.

The proof proceeds by transfinite induction on the size of
I
.

•

Base case:

I=\emptyset
, so clearly:

\sup_{x\in D}\sum_{i\in\emptyset}f_{i}(x)=\sup_{x\in D}\dsser{0}=\dsser{0}=\sum_{i\in\emptyset}\sup_{x\in D}f_{i}(x)

•

Successor Case:
Suppose the claim holds for sets of size
\alpha
, and let
|I|=\alpha+1
. We can partition
I
into
I^{\prime}\cup\{i\}
where
i\in I
is an arbitrary element and
I^{\prime}=I\setminus\{i\}
so that
|I^{\prime}|=\alpha
. Now, we have that:

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in D}\sum_{j\in I}f_{j}(x)

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in D}(\sum_{j\in I^{\prime}}f_{j}(x))+f_{i}(x)

Now, note that since all the
f_{j}
functions are Scott continuous, they must also be monotone, and addition is also monotone. Therefore the following equality holds
(
Abramsky and Jung 1995
, Proposition 2.1.12)
.

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in D}\sup_{y\in D}(\sum_{j\in I^{\prime}}f_{j}(x))+f_{i}(y)

By continuity of the semiring:

\displaystyle=(\sup_{x\in D}\sum_{j\in I^{\prime}}f_{j}(x))+(\sup_{y\in D}f_{i}(y))

By continuity of
f_{i}
and the induction hypothesis:

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in I^{\prime}}f_{j}(\sup D)+f_{i}(\sup D)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}(\sup D)

•

Limit case:
suppose that the claim holds for all finite index sets. Now, given the definition of the sum operator:

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in D}\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}(x)

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in D}\sup_{J\subseteq_{\mathsf{fin}}I}\sum_{j\in J}f_{j}(x)

The finite subsets of
I
are a directed set and clearly the inner sum is monotone in
x
and
J
, so we can rearrange the suprema
(
Abramsky and Jung 1995
, Proposition 2.1.12)
.

\displaystyle=\sup_{J\subseteq_{\mathsf{fin}}I}\sup_{x\in D}\sum_{j\in J}f_{j}(x)

By the induction hypothesis:

\displaystyle=\sup_{J\subseteq_{\mathsf{fin}}I}\sum_{j\in J}f_{j}(\sup D)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}(\sup D)

∎

Lemma A.2.

If
\sum_{i\in I}u_{i}
is defined, then for any
(v_{i})_{i\in I}
,
\sum_{i\in I}u_{i}\cdot v_{i}
is defined.

Proof.

Let
v
be the top element of
U
, so
v\geq v_{i}
for all
i\in I
. That means that for each
i\in I
, there is a
v_{i}^{\prime}
such that
v_{i}+v^{\prime}_{i}=v
. Now, since multiplication is total, then we know that
(\sum_{i\in I}u_{i})\cdot v
is defined. This gives us:

(\sum_{i\in I}u_{i})\cdot v=\sum_{i\in I}u_{i}\cdot(v_{i}+v^{\prime}_{i})=\sum_{i\in I}u_{i}\cdot v_{i}+\sum_{i\in I}u_{i}\cdot v^{\prime}_{i}

And since
\sum_{i\in I}u_{i}\cdot v_{i}
is a subexpression of the above well-defined term, then it must be well-defined.
∎

Lemma A.3.

For any
m\in\mathcal{W}(X)
and
f\colon X\to\mathcal{W}(Y)
, we get that
f^{\dagger}\colon\mathcal{W}(X)\to\mathcal{W}(Y)
is a total function.

Proof.

First, recall the definition of
(-)^{\dagger}
:

f^{\dagger}(m)(y)=\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(x)\cdot f(x)(y)

To show that this is well-defined, we need to show both that the sum exists, and that the resulting weighting function has a well-defined mass. First, we remark that since
m\in\mathcal{W}(A)
, then
|m|=\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(x)
must be defined. By
Lemma

A.2
, the sum in the definition of
(-)^{\dagger}
is therefore defined. Now, we need to show that
|f^{\dagger}(m)|
is defined:

\displaystyle|f^{\dagger}(m)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(f^{\dagger}(m))}f^{\dagger}(m)(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\bigcup_{a\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}\mathsf{supp}(f(a))}\;\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(x)\cdot f(x)(y)

By commutativity and associativity:

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(x)\;\cdot\;\sum_{y\in\mathsf{supp}(f(x))}f(x)(y)=\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(x)\cdot|f(x)|

Now, since
f(x)\in\mathcal{W}(Y)
for all
x\in X
, we know that
|f(x)|
must be defined. The outer sum also must be defined by
Lemma

A.2
.
∎

In the following, when comparing functions
f,g\colon X\to\mathcal{W}(Y)
, we will use the pointwise order. That is,
f\sqsubseteq^{\bullet}g
iff
f(x)\sqsubseteq g(x)
for all
x\in X
.

Lemma A.4.

(-)^{\dagger}\colon(X\to\mathcal{W}(Y))\to(\mathcal{W}(X)\to\mathcal{W}(Y))
is Scott continuous.

Proof.

Let
D\subseteq(X\to\mathcal{W}(Y))
be a directed set.
First, we show that for any
x\in X
, the function
g(f)=m(x)\cdot f(x)(y)
is Scott continuous:

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in D}g(f)

\displaystyle=\sup_{f\in D}m(x)\cdot f(x)(y)

By Scott continuity of the
\cdot
operator:

\displaystyle=m(x)\cdot\sup_{f\in D}f(x)(y)

Since we are using the pointwise ordering:

\displaystyle=m(x)\cdot(\sup D)(x)(y)=g(\sup D)

Now, we show that
(-)^{\dagger}
is Scott continuous. For any
m
, we have:

\displaystyle(\sup_{f\in D}f^{\dagger})(m)

\displaystyle=\sup_{f\in D}f^{\dagger}(m)

\displaystyle=\sup_{f\in D}\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(x)\cdot f(x)

By
Lemma

A.1
, using the property we just proved.

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(x)\cdot(\sup D)(x)=(\sup D)^{\dagger}(m)

∎

Lemma A.5.

Let
\Phi_{\langle C,e,e^{\prime}\rangle}(f)(\sigma)=\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot f^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(\sigma))+\left\llbracket e^{\prime}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot\eta(\sigma)
and suppose that it is a total function, then
\Phi_{\langle C,e,e^{\prime}\rangle}
is Scott continuous with respect to the pointwise order:
f_{1}\sqsubseteq^{\bullet}f_{2}
iff
f_{1}(\sigma)\sqsubseteq f_{2}(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
.

Proof.

For all directed sets
D\subseteq(\Sigma\to\mathcal{W}(\Sigma))
and
\sigma\in\Sigma
, we have:

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in D}\Phi_{\langle C,e,e^{\prime}\rangle}(f)(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\sup_{f\in D}\left(\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot f^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(\sigma))+\left\llbracket e^{\prime}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot\eta(\sigma)\right)

By the continuity of
+
and
\cdot
, we can move the supremum up to the
(-)^{\dagger}
, which is the only term that depends on
f
.

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot(\sup_{f\in D}f^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(\sigma)))+\left\llbracket e^{\prime}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot\eta(\sigma)

By
Lemma

A.4
.

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot(\sup D)^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(\sigma))+\left\llbracket e^{\prime}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot\eta(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{\langle C,e,e^{\prime}\rangle}(\sup D)(\sigma)

Since this is true for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
,

\Phi_{\langle C,e,e^{\prime}\rangle}
is Scott continuous.
∎

Now, given
Lemma

A.5
and the Kleene fixed point theorem, we know that the least fixed point is defined and is equal to:

\mathsf{lfp}\left(\Phi_{\langle C,e,e^{\prime}\rangle}\right)=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\Phi^{n}_{\langle C,e,e^{\prime}\rangle}(\lambda\tau.\dsser{0})

Therefore the semantics of iteration loops is well-defined, assuming that
\Phi_{\langle C,e,e^{\prime}\rangle}
is total. In the next section, we will see simple syntactic conditions to ensure this.

A.3.
Syntactic Sugar

If a partial semiring is used to interpret the language semantics, then unrestricted use of the
C_{1}+C_{2}
and
{C}^{\langle e,~e^{\prime}\rangle}
constructs may be undefined. In this section, we give some sufficient conditions to ensure that program semantics is well-defined. This is based on the notion of compatible expressions, introduced below.

Definition A.6 (Compatibility).

The expressions
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are compatible in semiring
\mathcal{A}=\langle U,+,\cdot,\dsser{0},\dsser{1}\rangle
if
\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)+\left\llbracket e_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)
is defined for any
\sigma\in\Sigma
.

The nondeterministic (
Examples

3.7
and
3.9
) and tropical (
Example

3.11
) instances use total semirings, so any program has well-defined semantics. In other
interpretations, we must ensure that programs are well-defined by ensuring that all uses of choice and iteration use compatible expressions. We begin by showing that any two collections can be combined if they are scaled by compatible expressions.

Lemma A.7.

If
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are compatible, then
\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot m_{1}+\left\llbracket e_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot m_{2}
is defined for any
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
.

Proof.

Since
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are compatible, then
\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)+\left\llbracket e_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)
is defined. By
Lemma

A.2
, that also means that
\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot|m_{1}|+\left\llbracket e_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot|m_{2}|
is defined too. Now, we have:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot|m_{1}|+\left\llbracket e_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot|m_{2}|

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}m_{1}(\tau)+\left\llbracket e_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}m_{2}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})}\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot m_{1}(\tau)+\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}\left\llbracket e_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot m_{2}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{1})\cup\mathsf{supp}(m_{2})}\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot m_{1}(\tau)+\left\llbracket e_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot m_{2}(\tau)

\displaystyle=|\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot m_{1}+\left\llbracket e_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot m_{2}|

Therefore
\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot m_{1}+\left\llbracket e_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot m_{2}
must be well-defined.
∎

Now, we show how this result relates to program semantics. We begin with branching, by showing that guarding the two branches using compatible expressions yields a program that is well-defined.

Lemma A.8.

If
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are compatible and
\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket
and
\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket
are total functions, then:

\left\llbracket(\texttt{assume}~e_{1}\fatsemi C_{1})+(\texttt{assume}~e_{2}\fatsemi C_{2})\right\rrbracket

is a total function.

Proof.

Take any
\sigma\in\Sigma
, then we have:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket(\texttt{assume}~e_{1}\fatsemi C_{1})+(\texttt{assume}~e_{2}\fatsemi C_{2})\right\rrbracket(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\left\llbracket\texttt{assume}~e_{1}\right\rrbracket(\sigma))+\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\left\llbracket\texttt{assume}~e_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma))

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot\eta(\sigma))+\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\left\llbracket e_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot\eta(\sigma))

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\eta(\sigma))+\left\llbracket e_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\eta(\sigma))

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)+\left\llbracket e_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)

By
Lemma

A.7
, we know that this sum is defined, therefore the semantics is valid.
∎

For iteration, we can similarly use compatibility to ensure well-definedness.

Lemma A.9.

If
e
and
e^{\prime}
are compatible and
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket
is a total function, then
\left\llbracket{C}^{\langle e,~e^{\prime}\rangle}\right\rrbracket
is a total function.

Proof.

Let
\Phi_{\langle C,e,e^{\prime}\rangle}(f)(\sigma)=\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot f^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(\sigma))+\left\llbracket e^{\prime}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot\eta(\sigma)
. Since
e
and
e^{\prime}
are compatible, it follows from
Lemma

A.7
that
\Phi_{\langle C,e,e^{\prime}\rangle}
is a total function. By
Lemma

A.5
, we therefore also know that
\left\llbracket{C}^{\langle e,~e^{\prime}\rangle}\right\rrbracket
is total.
∎

To conclude, we will provide a few examples of compatible expressions. For any test
b
, it is easy to see that
b
and
\lnot b
are compatible. This is because at any state
\sigma
, one of
\left\llbracket b\right\rrbracket(\sigma)
or
\left\llbracket\lnot b\right\rrbracket(\sigma)
must be
\dsser{0}
, and given the semiring laws,
\dsser{0}+u
is defined for any
u\in U
. Given this, our encodings of if statements and while loops from
Section

3.4
are well-defined in all interpretations.

In the probabilistic interpretation (
Example

3.10
), the weights
p
and
1-p
are compatible for any
p\in[0,1]
. That means that our encoding of probabilistic choice
C_{1}+_{p}C_{2}
and probabilistic iteration
C^{\langle p\rangle}
are both well-defined too.

Appendix B
Soundness and Completeness of Outcome Logic

We provide a formal definition of assertion entailment
\varphi\vDash e=u
, which informally means that
\varphi
has enough information to determine that the expression
e
evaluates to the value
u
.

Definition B.1 (Assertion Entailment).

Given an outcome assertion
\varphi
, an expression
e
, and a weight
u\in U
, we define the following:

\varphi\vDash e=u\qquad\text{iff}\qquad\forall m\in\varphi,\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m).\quad\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=u

Note that this can generally be considered equivalent to
\varphi\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(e=u)
, however we did not assume that equality is in the set of primitive tests.
Occasionally we will also write
\varphi\vDash b
for some test
b
, which is shorthand for
\varphi\vDash b=\dsser{1}
. It is relatively easy to see that the following statements hold given this definition:

\begin{array}[]{rllll}\top&\vDash&e=u&\text{iff}&\forall\sigma\in\Sigma.\quad\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=u\\
\bot&\vDash&e=u&\lx@intercol\text{always}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\varphi\lor\psi&\vDash&e=u&\text{iff}&\varphi\vDash e=u\quad\text{and}\quad\psi\vDash e=u\\
\varphi\land\psi&\vDash&e=u&\text{iff}&\varphi\vDash e=u\quad\text{or}\quad\psi\vDash e=u\\
\varphi\oplus\psi&\vDash&e=u&\text{iff}&\varphi\vDash e=u\quad\text{and}\quad\psi\vDash e=u\\
\varphi\odot{v}&\vDash&e=u&\text{iff}&v=\dsser{0}\quad\text{or}\quad\varphi\vDash e=u\\
v\odot\varphi&\vDash&e=u&\text{iff}&v=\dsser{0}\quad\text{or}\quad\varphi\vDash e=u\\
\mathbf{1}_{m}&\vDash&e=u&\text{iff}&\forall\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m).\quad\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=u\\
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(v)}&\vDash&e=u&\text{iff}&v=\dsser{0}\quad\text{or}\quad\forall\sigma\in P.\quad\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=u\\
{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}P&\vDash&e=u&\text{iff}&P\vDash e=u\end{array}

We now present the soundness proof, following the sketch from
Section

4.4
. The first results pertain to the semantics of iteration. We start by recalling the characteristic function:

\Phi_{\langle C,e,e^{\prime}\rangle}(f)(\sigma)=\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot f^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(\sigma))+\left\llbracket e^{\prime}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot\eta(\sigma)

Note that, as defined in
Figure

1
,
\left\llbracket{C}^{\langle e,~e^{\prime}\rangle}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=\mathsf{lfp}\left(\Phi_{\langle C,e,e^{\prime}\rangle}\right)(\sigma)
. The first lemma relates
\Phi_{\langle C,e,e\rangle}
to a sequence of unrolled commands.

Lemma B.2.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

\Phi^{n+1}_{\langle C,e,e^{\prime}\rangle}(\lambda x.\dsser{0})=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\left\llbracket(\texttt{assume}~e\fatsemi C)^{k}\fatsemi\texttt{assume}~e^{\prime}\right\rrbracket

Proof.

By mathematical induction on
n
.

•

n=0
. Unfolding the definition of
\Phi_{\langle C,e_{1},e_{2}\rangle}
, for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
we get:

\displaystyle\Phi_{\langle C,e,e^{\prime}\rangle}(\lambda x.\dsser{0})(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot(\lambda x.\dsser{0})^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(\sigma))+\left\llbracket e^{\prime}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot\eta(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\dsser{0}+\left\llbracket e^{\prime}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot\eta(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket\texttt{assume}~e^{\prime}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket(\texttt{assume}~e\fatsemi C)^{0}\fatsemi\texttt{assume}~e^{\prime}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)

•

Inductive step, suppose the claim holds for
n
. Now, for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
:

\displaystyle\Phi^{n+2}_{\langle C,e,e^{\prime}\rangle}(\lambda x.\dsser{0})(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot\Phi^{n+1}_{(\langle b,C\rangle)}(\lambda x.\dsser{0})^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(\sigma))+\left\llbracket e^{\prime}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot\eta(\sigma)

By the induction hypothesis

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot\left(\sum_{k=0}^{n}\left\llbracket(\texttt{assume}~e\fatsemi C)^{k}\fatsemi\texttt{assume}~e^{\prime}\right\rrbracket\right)^{\dagger}\!\!\!(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(\sigma))+\left\llbracket e^{\prime}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot\eta(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}\left\llbracket(\texttt{assume}~e\fatsemi C)^{k}\fatsemi\texttt{assume}~e^{\prime}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)+\left\llbracket\texttt{assume}~e^{\prime}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n+1}\left\llbracket(\texttt{assume}~e\fatsemi C)^{k}\fatsemi\texttt{assume}~e^{\prime}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)

∎

See
4.4

Proof.

First, by the Kleene fixed point theorem and the program semantics (
Figure

1
), we get:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{C}^{\langle e,~e^{\prime}\rangle}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\Phi^{n}_{\langle C,e,e^{\prime}\rangle}(\lambda x.\dsser{0})(\sigma)

Now, since
\Phi^{0}_{\langle C,e,e^{\prime}\rangle}(\lambda x.\dsser{0})(\sigma)=\dsser{0}
and
\dsser{0}
is the bottom of the order
\sqsubseteq
, we can rewrite the supremum as follows.

\displaystyle=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\Phi^{n+1}_{\langle C,e,e^{\prime}\rangle}(\lambda x.\dsser{0})(\sigma)

By
Lemma

B.2
:

\displaystyle=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\left\llbracket(\texttt{assume}~e\fatsemi C)^{k}\fatsemi\texttt{assume}~e^{\prime}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)

By the definition of infinite sums:

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left\llbracket(\texttt{assume}~e\fatsemi C)^{n}\fatsemi\texttt{assume}~e^{\prime}\right\rrbracket(\sigma)

∎

See
4.3

Proof.

The triple
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
is proven using inference rules from
Figure

3
, or by applying an axiom in
\Omega
. If the last step is using an axiom, then the proof is trivial since we assumed that all the axioms in
\Omega
are semantically valid. If not, then the proof is by induction on the derivation
\Omega\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
.

•

Skip
. We need to show that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{skip}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
.
Suppose that
m\vDash\varphi
. Since
\left\llbracket\texttt{skip}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)=m
, then clearly
\left\llbracket\texttt{skip}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\vDash\varphi
.

•

Seq
. Given that
\Omega\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\vartheta{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
and
\Omega\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\vartheta{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
, we need to show that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
.
Note that since
\Omega\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\vartheta{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
and
\Omega\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\vartheta{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
, those triples must be derived either using inference rules (in which case the induction hypothesis applies), or by applying an axiom in
\Omega
. In either case, we can conclude that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\vartheta{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
and
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\vartheta{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
.
Suppose that
m\vDash\varphi
. Since
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\vartheta{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
, we get that
\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\vDash\vartheta
and using
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\vartheta{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
, we get that
\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m))\vDash\psi
. Since
\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m))=(\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\circ\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket)^{\dagger}(m)=\left\llbracket C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)
, we are done.

•

Plus
. Given
\Omega\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
and
\Omega\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
, we need to show
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}+C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{1}\oplus\psi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
. Suppose that
m\vDash\varphi
, so by the induction hypotheses,
\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\vDash\psi_{1}
and
\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\vDash\psi_{2}
. Recall from the remark at the end of
Section

3.4
that we are assuming that programs are well-formed, and therefore
\left\llbracket C_{1}+C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)
is defined and it is equal to
\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)+\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)
. Therefore by the semantics of
\oplus
,
\left\llbracket C_{1}+C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\vDash\psi_{1}\oplus\psi_{2}
.

•

Assume
. Given
\varphi\vDash e=u
, we must show
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{assume}~e~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi\odot u{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
. Suppose
m\vDash\varphi
. Since
\varphi\vDash e=u
, then
\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)=u
for all
\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m)
. This means that:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\texttt{assume}~e\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(\sigma)\cdot\left\llbracket\texttt{assume}~e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(\sigma)\cdot\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket(\sigma)\cdot\eta(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(\sigma)\cdot u\cdot\eta(\sigma)

\displaystyle=(\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(\sigma)\cdot\eta(\sigma))\cdot u

\displaystyle=m\cdot u

And by definition,
m\cdot u\vDash\varphi\odot u
, so we are done.

•

Iter
. We know that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{assume}~e\fatsemi C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{n+1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
and that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{assume}~e^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
by the induction hypotheses. Now, we need to show that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{0}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~{C}^{\langle e,~e^{\prime}\rangle}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{\infty}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
. Suppose
m\vDash\varphi_{0}
. It is easy to see that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

\left\llbracket(\texttt{assume}~e\fatsemi C)^{n}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\vDash\varphi_{n}

and

\left\llbracket(\texttt{assume}~e\fatsemi C)^{n}\fatsemi\texttt{assume}~e^{\prime}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\vDash\psi_{n}

by mathematical induction on
n
, and the two induction hypotheses. Now, since
({\psi}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\rightsquigarrow{\psi}_{\infty}
, we also know that:

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left\llbracket(\texttt{assume}~e\fatsemi C)^{n}\fatsemi\texttt{assume}~e^{\prime}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\vDash\psi_{\infty}

Finally, by
Lemma

4.4
we get that
\left\llbracket{C}^{\langle e,~e^{\prime}\rangle}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\vDash\psi_{\infty}
.

•

False
. We must show that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bot{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
. Suppose that
m\vDash\bot
. This is impossible, so the claim follows vacuously.

•

True
. We must show that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\top{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
. Suppose
m\vDash\varphi
. It is trivial that
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\vDash\top
, so the triple is valid.

•

Scale
. By the induction hypothesis, we get that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
and we must show that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}u\odot\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}u\odot\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
. Suppose
m\vDash u\odot\varphi
. So there is some
m^{\prime}
such that
m^{\prime}\vDash\varphi
and
m=u\cdot m^{\prime}
. We therefore get that
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m^{\prime})\vDash\psi
. Now, observe that
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)=\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(u\cdot m^{\prime})=u\cdot\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)
. Finally, by the definition of
\odot
, we get that
u\cdot\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\vDash u\odot\psi
.

•

Disj
. By the induction hypothesis, we know that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
and
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
and we need to show
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{1}\vee\psi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
. Suppose
m\vDash\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}
. Without loss of generality, suppose
m\vDash\varphi_{1}
. By the induction hypothesis, we get
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\vDash\psi_{1}
. We can weaken this to conclude that
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\vDash\psi_{1}\vee\psi_{2}
. The case where instead
m\vDash\varphi_{2}
is symmteric.

•

Conj
. By the induction hypothesis, we get that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
and
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
and we need to show
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
. Suppose
m\vDash\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}
, so
m\vDash\varphi_{1}
and
m\vDash\varphi_{2}
. By the induction hypotheses,
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\vDash\psi_{1}
and
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\vDash\psi_{2}
, so
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\vDash\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2}
.

•

Choice
. By the induction hypothesis,
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\phi(t){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\phi^{\prime}(t){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
for all
t\in T
, and we need to show that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{x\in T}\phi(x){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{x\in T}\phi^{\prime}(x){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
. Suppose
m\vDash\bigoplus_{x\in T}\phi(x)
, so for each
t\in T
there is an
m_{t}
such that
m_{t}\in\phi(t)
and
m=\sum_{t\in T}m_{t}
. By the induction hypothesis, we get that
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m_{t})\in\phi^{\prime}(t)
for all
t\in T
. Now, we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{t\in T}\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\in T}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{t})}m_{t}(\sigma)\cdot\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}(\sum_{t\in T}m_{t}(\sigma))\cdot\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}m(\sigma)\cdot\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)

Therefore, we get that
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\in\bigoplus_{x\in T}\phi^{\prime}(x)
.

•

Exists
. By the induction hypothesis,
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\phi(t){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\phi^{\prime}(t){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
for all
t\in T
and we need to show
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\exists x:T.\phi(x){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\exists x:T.\phi^{\prime}(x){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
. Now suppose
m\in\exists x:T.\phi(x)=\bigcup_{t\in T}\phi(t)
. This means that there is some
t\in T
such that
m\in\phi(t)
. By the induction hypothesis, this means that
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\vDash\phi^{\prime}(t)
, so we get that
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\vDash\exists x:T.\phi^{\prime}(x)
.

•

Consequence
. We know that
\varphi^{\prime}\Rightarrow\varphi
and
\psi\Rightarrow\psi^{\prime}
and by the induction hypothesis
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
, and we need to show that
\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
. Suppose that
m\vDash\varphi^{\prime}
, then it also must be the case that
m\vDash\varphi
. By the induction hypothesis,
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\vDash\psi
. Now, using the second consequence
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\vDash\psi^{\prime}
.

∎

Now, moving to completeness, we prove the following lemma.
See
4.6

Proof.

By induction on the structure of the program
C
.

•

C=\texttt{skip}
. Since
\left\llbracket\texttt{skip}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)=m
for all
m
, then clearly
\mathsf{post}(\texttt{skip},\varphi)=\varphi
. We complete the proof by applying the
Skip
rule.

\frac{\;}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{skip}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Skip}{{Skip}}}

•

C=C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2}
. First, observe that:

\displaystyle\mathsf{post}(C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2},\varphi)

\displaystyle=\{\left\llbracket C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\mid m\in\varphi\}

\displaystyle=\{\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m))\mid m\in\varphi\}

\displaystyle=\{\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m^{\prime})\mid m^{\prime}\in\{\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\mid m\in\varphi\}\}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{post}(C_{2},\mathsf{post}(C_{1},\varphi))

Now, by the induction hypothesis, we know that:

\displaystyle\Omega\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathsf{post}(C_{1},\varphi){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

\displaystyle\Omega\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathsf{post}(C_{1},\varphi){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathsf{post}(C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2},\varphi){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

Now, we complete the derivation as follows:

\frac{\frac{\Omega}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathsf{post}(C_{1},\varphi){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\frac{\Omega}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathsf{post}(C_{1},\varphi){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathsf{post}(C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2},\varphi){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathsf{post}(C_{1}\fatsemi C_{2},\varphi){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Seq}{{Seq}}}

•

C=C_{1}+C_{2}
. So, we have that:

\displaystyle\mathsf{post}(C_{1}+C_{2},\varphi)

\displaystyle=\{\left\llbracket C_{1}+C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\mid m\in\varphi\}

\displaystyle=\{\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)+\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\mid m\in\varphi\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{m\in\varphi}\{\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)+\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)\mid m\in\mathbf{1}_{m}\}

\displaystyle=\exists m:\varphi.~\mathsf{post}(C_{1},\mathbf{1}_{m})\oplus\mathsf{post}(C_{2},\mathbf{1}_{m})

We now complete the derivation as follows:
