and therefore we can weaken the assertion to conclude that
m^{\prime}\vDash\varphi_{1}\lor\varphi_{2}
.

•

\varphi=\forall y:T.\ \psi
. We know that
m\in\mathcal{H}^{S}_{x}[\psi[v/y]]
for all
v\in T
, therefore by the induction hypothesis,
m^{\prime}\vDash\varphi[v/y]
for all
v\in T
. This means that
m^{\prime}\vDash\forall y\colon T.\ \varphi
.

•

\varphi=\exists y:T.\ \psi
. We know that
m\in\mathcal{H}^{S}_{x}[\psi[v/y]]
for some
v\in T
, therefore by the induction hypothesis,
m^{\prime}\vDash\varphi[v/y]
. This means that
m^{\prime}\vDash\exists y\colon T.\ \varphi
.

•

\varphi=\forall\langle\sigma\rangle.\ \psi
. We know that
m\in\mathcal{H}_{x}^{S}[\psi[v/\sigma(x)][s/\sigma]]
for all
s\in\mathsf{supp}(m)
and
v\in S
. Therefore, by the induction hypothesis,
m^{\prime}\vDash\psi[v/\sigma(x)][s/\sigma]
. Now, note that
\psi[v/\sigma(x)][s/\sigma]=\psi[s[x\coloneqq v]/\sigma]
, so clearly
m^{\prime}\vDash\psi[s[x\coloneqq v]/\sigma]
for each
s\in\mathsf{supp}(m)
and
v\in S
. In addition,
\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime})=\{s[x\coloneqq v]\mid s\in\mathsf{supp}(m),v\in S\}
, and so
m^{\prime}\vDash\psi[t/\sigma]
for each
t\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime})
, therefore
m^{\prime}\vDash\forall\langle\sigma\rangle.\ \psi
.

•

\varphi=\exists\langle\sigma\rangle.\ \psi
. We know that
m\in\mathcal{H}_{x}^{S}[\psi[v/\sigma(x)][s/\sigma]]
for some
s\in\mathsf{supp}(m)
and
v\in S
. Therefore, by the induction hypothesis,
m^{\prime}\vDash\psi[v/\sigma(x)][s/\sigma]
. Now, note that
\psi[v/\sigma(x)][s/\sigma]=\psi[s[x\coloneqq v]/\sigma]
, so clearly
m^{\prime}\vDash\psi[s[x\coloneqq v]/\sigma]
for some
s\in\mathsf{supp}(m)
and
v\in S
. In addition,
\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime})=\{s[x\coloneqq v]\mid s\in\mathsf{supp}(m),v\in S\}
, and so
m^{\prime}\vDash\psi[t/\sigma]
for some
t\in\mathsf{supp}(m^{\prime})
, therefore
m^{\prime}\vDash\exists\langle\sigma\rangle.\ \psi
.

•

\varphi=B
. If
m\in\mathcal{H}^{S}_{x}[B]=B
, then
B
must be a tautology, so it must be the case that
m^{\prime}\vDash B
.

∎

Lemma F.3.

The following rule is derivable

\frac{\;}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathcal{H}_{x}^{\{a,b\}}[\varphi]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~(x\coloneqq a)+(x\coloneqq b)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}

Proof.

\frac{\forall m\in\mathcal{H}^{\{a,b\}}_{x}[\varphi].\frac{\frac{\frac{\;}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathbf{1}_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\coloneqq a~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\llbracket x=a\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Assign}{{Assign}}}\hskip 7.3612pt\frac{\;}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathbf{1}_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\coloneqq b~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\llbracket x=b\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Assign}{{Assign}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathbf{1}_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~(x\coloneqq a)+(x\coloneqq b)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bigoplus_{v\in\{a,b\}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\llbracket x=v\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Plus}{{Plus}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathbf{1}_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~(x\coloneqq a)+(x\coloneqq b)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{{Consequence}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathcal{H}_{x}^{\{a,b\}}[\varphi]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~(x\coloneqq a)+(x\coloneqq b)~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Exists}{{Exists}}}

∎

Lemma F.4.

The following rule is derivable

\frac{\;}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathcal{H}_{x}^{\mathbb{N}}[\varphi]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\coloneqq\bigstar~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}

Proof.

Recall that
x\coloneqq\bigstar
is syntactic sugar for
x\coloneqq 0\fatsemi(x\coloneqq x+1)^{\star}
and
(x\coloneqq x+1)^{\star}
is syntactic sugar for
{(x\coloneqq x+1)}^{\langle\dsser{1},~\dsser{1}\rangle}
.
We will derive this rule using the
Iter
rule. The first step is to select the assertion families:

\varphi_{n}\triangleq\psi_{n}\triangleq\mathbf{1}_{\left\llbracket x\coloneqq n\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)}\qquad\psi_{\infty}\triangleq\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\llbracket x\coloneqq k\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)}

So clearly
({\psi}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\rightsquigarrow{\psi}_{\infty}
. We now complete derivation
(1)
below:

\frac{\frac{\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\left\llbracket x\coloneqq n\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)}\vDash\dsser{1}=\dsser{1}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\llbracket x\coloneqq n\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{assume}~\dsser{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\llbracket x\coloneqq n\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Assume}{{Assume}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\frac{\;}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\llbracket x\coloneqq n\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\coloneqq x+1~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\llbracket x\coloneqq n+1\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Assign}{{Assign}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\llbracket x\coloneqq n\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{assume}~\dsser{1}\fatsemi x\coloneqq x+1~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\llbracket x\coloneqq n+1\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Seq}{{Seq}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\llbracket x\coloneqq n\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~(x\coloneqq x+1)^{\star}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\llbracket x\coloneqq k\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Iter}{{Iter}}}

Using
(1)
above, we complete the derivation as follows:

\frac{\forall m\in\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{N}}_{x}[\varphi].\quad\frac{\frac{\frac{\;}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathbf{1}_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\coloneqq 0~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\llbracket x\coloneqq 0\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Assign}{{Assign}}}\hskip 7.3612pt(1)}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathbf{1}_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\coloneqq\bigstar~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\textstyle\bigoplus_{v\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\llbracket x=v\right\rrbracket^{\dagger}\!(m)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Seq}{{Seq}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathbf{1}_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\coloneqq\bigstar~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{{Consequence}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\mathcal{H}_{x}^{\mathbb{N}}[\varphi]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~x\coloneqq\bigstar~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Exists}{{Exists}}}

∎

F.3.
Assume

\displaystyle\Pi_{b}[\varphi\land\psi]

\displaystyle\triangleq\Pi_{b}[\varphi]\land\Pi_{b}[\psi]

\displaystyle\Pi_{b}[\varphi\lor\psi]

\displaystyle\triangleq\Pi_{b}[\varphi]\lor\Pi_{b}[\psi]

\displaystyle\Pi_{b}[\forall x\colon T.\varphi]

\displaystyle\triangleq\forall x\colon T.\Pi_{b}[\varphi]

\displaystyle\Pi_{b}[\exists x\colon T.\varphi]

\displaystyle\triangleq\exists x\colon T.\Pi_{b}[\varphi]

\displaystyle\Pi_{b}[\forall\langle\sigma\rangle.\varphi]

\displaystyle\triangleq\forall\langle\sigma\rangle.\ \lnot b[\sigma]\vee\Pi_{b}[\varphi]

\displaystyle\Pi_{b}[\exists\langle\sigma\rangle.\varphi]

\displaystyle\triangleq\exists\langle\sigma\rangle.\ b[\sigma]\land\Pi_{b}[\varphi]

\displaystyle\Pi_{b}[B]

\displaystyle\triangleq B

Lemma F.5.

For any assertion
\varphi
(from the syntax in
Section

6.2
) and test
b
,
\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\Pi_{b}[\varphi]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{assume}~b~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

Proof.

We first establish that for any
m
, if
m\vDash\Pi_{b}[\varphi]
, then
m\vDash(\varphi\land{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}b)\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(\lnot b)
by induction on the structure of
\varphi
.

•

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}
. We know that
m\vDash\Pi_{b}[\varphi_{i}]
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
. By the induction hypothesis, we get that
m\vDash(\varphi_{i}\land{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}b)\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(\lnot b)
, so
m\vDash((\varphi_{1}\land{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}b)\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(\lnot b))\land((\varphi_{2}\land{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}b)\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(\lnot b))
. Given that
b
and
\lnot b
are disjoint, we can simplify this to
m\vDash(\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}\land{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}b)\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(\lnot b)
.

•

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\lor\varphi_{2}
. Without loss of generality, suppose
m\vDash\Pi_{b}[\varphi_{1}]
. By the induction hypothesis, we get that
m\vDash(\varphi_{1}\land{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}b)\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(\lnot b)
. We can weaken this to
m\vDash((\varphi_{1}\lor\varphi_{2})\land{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}b)\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(\lnot b)
.

•

\varphi=\forall x\colon T.\varphi
. Similar to the case for conjunctions above.

•

\varphi=\exists x\colon T.\varphi
. Similar to the case for disjunctions above.

•

\varphi=\forall\langle\sigma\rangle.\varphi
. We know that
m\vDash\lnot b[s]\lor\Pi_{b}[\varphi[s/\sigma]]
for all
s\in\mathsf{supp}(m)
. So, for each
s
, we know that either
\left\llbracket b\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Test}}(s)=\mathsf{false}
, of
m\vDash\Pi_{b}[\varphi[s/\sigma]]
, in which case
m\vDash(\varphi[s/\sigma]\land{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}b)\oplus({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(\lnot b))
by the induction hypothesis. Since this is true for all
s
, we get
m\vDash((\forall\langle\sigma\rangle.\varphi)\land{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}b)\oplus({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(\lnot b))
.

•

\varphi=\exists\langle\sigma\rangle.\varphi
. We know that
m\vDash b[s]\land\Pi_{b}[\varphi[s/\sigma]]
for some
s\in\mathsf{supp}(m)
. So, by the induction hypothesis
m\vDash(\varphi[s/\sigma]\land{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}b)\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(\lnot b)
, which we can weaken to
m\vDash((\exists\langle\sigma\rangle.\varphi)\land{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}b)\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(\lnot b)

•

\varphi=B
. Since
m\vDash B
, then
B
must not contain any free variables, and therefore
B
is a tautology. So we have:

m\vDash\top\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad m\vDash{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}b\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(\lnot b)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad m\vDash(B\land{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}b)\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(\lnot b)

We complete the derivation as follows:

\frac{\frac{\frac{\varphi\land{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}b\vDash b}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi\land{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}b{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{assume}~b~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi\land{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}b{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Assume}{{Assume}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(\lnot b)\vDash b=\dsser{0}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(\lnot b){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{assume}~b~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(\lnot b))\odot\dsser{0}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Assume}{{Assume}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}(\varphi\land{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}b)\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(\lnot b){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{assume}~b~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}(\varphi\land{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}b)\oplus({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(\lnot b))\odot\dsser{0}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{Right=\hyperlink{rule:Choice}{{Choice}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\Pi_{b}[\varphi]{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{assume}~b~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}}\quad\textsc{right=\hyperlink{rule:Consequence}{{Consequence}}}

∎

Appendix G
Reusing Proof Fragments

G.1.
Integer Division

Recall the definition of the program below that divides two integers.

\texttt{Div}\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}q\coloneqq 0\fatsemi r\coloneqq a\fatsemi\\
\texttt{while}~r\geq b~\texttt{do}\\
\quad r\coloneqq r-b\fatsemi\\
\quad q\coloneqq q+1\end{array}\right.

To analyze this program with the
Variant
rule, we need a family of variants
(\varphi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, defined as follows.

\varphi_{n}\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q+n=\lfloor a\div b\rfloor\land r=(a\bmod b)+n\times b\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil&\text{if}&n\leq\lfloor a\div b\rfloor\\
\bot&\text{if}&n>\lfloor a\div b\rfloor\end{array}\right.

Additionally, it must be the case that
\varphi_{n}\vDash r\geq b
for all
n\geq 1
and
\varphi_{0}\vDash r<b
. For
n>\lfloor a\div b\rfloor
,
\varphi_{n}=\bot
and
\bot\vDash r\geq b
vacuously. If
1\leq n\leq\lfloor a\div b\rfloor
, then we know that
r=(a\bmod{b})+n\times b
, and since
n\geq 1
, then
r\geq b
. When
n=0
, we know that
r=a\bmod b
, and so by the definition of
\bmod
, it must be that
r<b
.

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a\geq 0\land b>0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;q\coloneqq 0\fatsemi\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a\geq 0\land b>0\land q=0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;r\coloneqq a\fatsemi\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a\geq 0\land b>0\land q=0\land r=a\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q+\lfloor a\div b\rfloor=\lfloor a\div b\rfloor\land r=(a\bmod{b})+\lfloor a\div b\rfloor\times b\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{\lfloor a\div b\rfloor}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\exists n:\mathbb{N}.\varphi_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\texttt{while}~r\geq b~\texttt{do}\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q+n=\lfloor a\div b\rfloor\land r=(a\bmod b)+n\times b\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\quad r\coloneqq r-b\fatsemi\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q+n=\lfloor a\div b\rfloor\land r=(a\bmod b)+(n-1)\times b\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\quad q\coloneqq q+1\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q+(n-1)=\lfloor a\div b\rfloor\land r=(a\bmod b)+(n-1)\times b\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{n-1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{0}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q+0=\lfloor a\div b\rfloor\land r=(a\bmod b)+0\times b\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q=\lfloor a\div b\rfloor\land r=(a\bmod b)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\end{array}

Figure 5
.
Derivation for the
\mathsf{DIV}
program.

The derivation is given in
Figure

5
. Most of the steps are obtained by straightforward applications of the inference rules, with consequences denoted by
\implies
. In the application of the
Variant
rule, we only show the case where
n\leq\lfloor a\div b\rfloor
. The case where
n>\lfloor a\div b\rfloor
is vacuous by applying the
False
rule from
Figure

3
.

G.2.
The Collatz Conjecture

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a=n\land n>0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;i\coloneqq 0\fatsemi\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a=n\land n>0\land i=0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a=f^{i}(n)\land\forall k<i.f^{k}(n)\neq 1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\texttt{while}~a\neq 1~\texttt{do}\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a=f^{i}(n)\land\forall k<i.f^{k}(n)\neq 1\land a\neq 1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a=f^{i}(n)\land\forall k<i+1.f^{k}(n)\neq 1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\quad b\coloneqq 2\fatsemi\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a=f^{i}(n)\land\forall k<i+1.f^{k}(n)\neq 1\land b=2\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\quad\texttt{Div}\fatsemi\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a=f^{i}(n)\land\forall k<i+1.f^{k}(n)\neq 1\land b=2\land q=\lfloor a\div b\rfloor\land r=(a\bmod b)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a=f^{i}(n)\land\forall k<i+1.f^{k}(n)\neq 1\land q=\lfloor f^{i}(n)\div 2\rfloor\land r=(f^{i}(n)\bmod 2)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\quad\texttt{if}~r=0~\texttt{then}\\
\quad\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a=f^{i}(n)\land\forall k<i+1.f^{k}(n)\neq 1\land q=\lfloor f^{i}(n)\div 2\rfloor\land r=(f^{i}(n)\bmod 2)\land r=0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
\quad\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\forall k<i+1.f^{k}(n)\neq 1\land q=\lfloor f^{i}(n)\div 2\rfloor\land(f^{i}(n)\bmod 2)=0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\quad\quad{a\coloneqq q}\\
\quad\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\forall k<i+1.f^{k}(n)\neq 1\land a=\lfloor f^{i}(n)\div 2\rfloor\land(f^{i}(n)\bmod 2)=0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
\quad\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(a=f^{i+1}(n)\land\forall k<i+1.f^{k}(n)\neq 1){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\quad\texttt{else}\\
\quad\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a=f^{i}(n)\land\forall k<i+1.f^{k}(n)\neq 1\land q=\lfloor f^{i}(n)\div 2\rfloor\land r=(f^{i}(n)\bmod 2)\land r\neq 0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
\quad\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a=f^{i}(n)\land\forall k<i+1.f^{k}(n)\neq 1\land(f^{i}(n)\bmod 2)=1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\quad\quad{a\coloneqq 3\times a+1}\fatsemi\\
\quad\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a=3\times f^{i}(n)+1\land\forall k<i+1.f^{k}(n)\neq 1\land(f^{i}(n)\bmod 2)=1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
\quad\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(a=f^{i+1}(n)\land\forall k<i+1.f^{k}(n)\neq 1){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(a=f^{i+1}(n)\land\forall k<i+1.f^{k}(n)\neq 1){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\quad i\coloneqq i+1\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(a=f^{i}(n)\land\forall k<i.f^{k}(n)\neq 1){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(a=f^{i}(n)\land\forall k<i.f^{k}(n)\neq 1\land a=1){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}(i=S_{n}){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\end{array}

Figure 6
.
Derivation for the
\mathsf{COLLATZ}
program.

Recall the definition of the program below that finds the stopping time of some positive number
n
.

\texttt{Collatz}\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}i\coloneqq 0\fatsemi\\
\texttt{while}~a\neq 1~\texttt{do}\\
\quad b\coloneqq 2\fatsemi\texttt{Div}\fatsemi\\
\quad\texttt{if}~r=0~\texttt{then}~a\coloneqq q~\texttt{else}~a\coloneqq 3\times a+1\fatsemi\\
\quad i\coloneqq i+1\end{array}\right.

The derivation is shown in
Figure

6
.
Since we do not know if the program will terminate, we use the
Invariant
rule to obtain a partial correctness specification. We choose the loop invariant:

a=f^{i}(n)\quad\land\quad\forall k<i.\ f^{k}(n)\neq 1

So, on each iteration of the loop,
a
holds the value of applying
f
repeatedly
i
times to
n
, and 1 has not yet appeared in this sequence.

Immediately upon entering the while loop, we see that
a=f^{i}(n)\neq 1
, and so from that and the fact that
\forall k<i.f^{k}(n)\neq 1
, we can conclude that
\forall k<i+1.f^{k}(n)\neq 1
.

The
\mathsf{DIV}
program is analyzed by inserting the proof from
Figure

5
, along with an application of the rule of
Constancy
to add information about the other variables. We can omit the
{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}
modality from rule of
Constancy
, since
\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\land{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\Box}Q\Leftrightarrow\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P\land Q\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
.

When it comes time to analyze the if statement, we use the
If (Hoare)
rule (
Lemma

E.8
) to get a partial correctness specification. The structure of the if statement mirrors the definition of
f(n)
, so the effect is the same as applying
f
to
a
one more time, therefore we get that
a=f^{i+1}(n)
.

After exiting the while loop, we know that
f^{i}(n)=1
and
f^{k}(n)\neq 1
for all
k<i
, therefore
i
is (by definition) the stopping time,
S_{n}
.

G.3.
Embedding Division in a Probabilistic Program

Recall the program that loops an even number of iterations with probability
\frac{2}{3}
and an odd number of iterations with probability
\frac{1}{3}
.

a:=0\fatsemi r:=0\fatsemi\left(a:=a+1\fatsemi b:=2\fatsemi\texttt{Div}\right)^{\langle\frac{1}{2}\rangle}

To analyze this program with the
Iter
rule, we define the two families of assertions below for
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

\varphi_{n}\triangleq\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a=n\land r=a\bmod 2\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\frac{1}{2^{n}})}\qquad\psi_{n}\triangleq\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}a=n\land r=a\bmod 2\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\frac{1}{2^{n+1}})}

Additionally, let
\psi_{\infty}=\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r=0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{2}{3}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}r=1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
. We now show that
(\psi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{\infty}}
converges. Suppose that
m_{n}\vDash\psi_{n}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. So
m_{n}\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r=0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\frac{1}{2^{n+1}})}
for all even
n
and
m_{n}\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r=1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\frac{1}{2^{n+1}})}
for all odd
n
. In other words, the cummulative probability mass for each
m_{n}
where
n
is even is:

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{2^{2k+1}}=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{k}=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{4}}=\frac{2}{3}

Where the second-to-last step is obtained using the standard formula for geometric series. Similarly, the total probability mass for
n
being odd is:

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{2^{2k+2}}=\frac{1}{4}\cdot\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{k}=\frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{4}}=\frac{1}{3}

We therefore get that
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}m_{n}\vDash\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r=0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{2}{3}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}r=1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil
. Having shown that, we complete the derivation, shown in
Figure

7
.
Two proof obligations are generated by applying the
Iter
rule, the first is proven in
Figure

7
. Note that in order to apply our previous proof for the
\mathsf{DIV}
program, it is necessary to use the
Scale
rule. The second proof obligation of the
Iter
rule is to show
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{assume}~\frac{1}{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}
, which is easily dispatched using the
Assume
rule.

\begin{array}[]{l|l}\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;a:=0\fatsemi\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a=0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;r:=0\fatsemi\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a=0\land r=0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a=0\land r=0\bmod 2\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(1)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{0}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{l}a:=a+1\fatsemi\\
b:=2\fatsemi\\
\texttt{Div}\end{array}\right)^{\langle\frac{1}{2}\rangle}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{\infty}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r=0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil\oplus_{\frac{2}{3}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}r=1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\end{array}&\quad\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a=n\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\frac{1}{2^{n}})}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\texttt{assume}~\frac{1}{2}\fatsemi\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a=n\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\frac{1}{2^{n+1}})}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;a:=a+1\fatsemi\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a=n+1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\frac{1}{2^{n+1}})}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;b:=2\fatsemi\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a=n+1\land b=2\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\frac{1}{2^{n+1}})}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\;\texttt{Div}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a=n+1\land r=a\bmod 2\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\frac{1}{2^{n+1}})}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{n+1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 7
.
Left: derivation for the main body of the probabilistic looping program. Right: derivation of the probabilistic loop.

Appendix H
Graph Problems and Quantitative Analysis

H.1.
Counting Random Walks

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=0\land y=0\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{N+M}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\exists n:\mathbb{N}.~\varphi_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\texttt{while}~x<N\vee y<M~\texttt{do}\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{n+1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\quad\texttt{if}~x<N\land y<M~\texttt{then}\\
\quad\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{k=\max(1,n+1-M)}^{\min(N,n)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=N-k\land y=M-(n+1-k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-(n+1)\choose N-k})}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\quad\quad(x\coloneqq x+1)+(y\coloneqq y+1)\\
\quad\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{k=\max(1,n+1-M)}^{\min(N,n)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=N-k+1\land y=M-(n+1-k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-(n+1)\choose N-k})}\oplus\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x=N-k\land y=M-(n-k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-(n+1)\choose N-k})}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\quad\texttt{else if}~x\geq N~\texttt{then}\\
\quad\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{k=\max(0,n+1-M)}^{0}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=N-k\land y=M-(n+1-k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-(n+1)\choose N-k})}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\quad\quad y\coloneqq y+1\\
\quad\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{k=\max(0,n+1-M)}^{0}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=N-k\land y=M-(n-k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-(n+1)\choose N-k})}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\quad\texttt{else}\\
\quad\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{k=n+1}^{\min(N,n+1)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=N-k\land y=M-(n+1-k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-(n+1)\choose N-k})}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\quad\quad x\coloneqq x+1\\
\quad\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{k=n+1}^{\min(N,n+1)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=N-k+1\land y=M-(n+1-k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-(n+1)\choose N-k})}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=N\land y=M\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M\choose N})}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\end{array}

Figure 8
.
Random walk proof

Recall the following program that performs a random walk on a two dimensional grid in order to discover how many paths exist between the origin
(0,0)
and the point
(N,M)
.

\texttt{Walk}\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{while}~x<N\vee y<M~\texttt{do}\\
\quad\texttt{if}~x<N\land y<M~\texttt{then}\\
\quad\quad(x\coloneqq x+1)+(y\coloneqq y+1)\\
\quad\texttt{else if}~x\geq N~\texttt{then}\\
\quad\quad y\coloneqq y+1\\
\quad\texttt{else}\\
\quad\quad x\coloneqq x+1\end{array}\right.

The derivation is provided in
Figure

8
.
Since this program is guaranteed to terminate after exactly
N+M
steps, we use the following loop
Variant
, where the bounds for
k
are described in
Section

8.1
.

\varphi_{n}\triangleq\bigoplus_{k=\max(0,n-M)}^{\min(N,n)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=N-k\land y=M-(n-k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-n\choose N-k})}

Recall that
n
indicates how many steps
(x,y)
is from
(N,M)
, so
\varphi_{N+M}
is the precondition and
\varphi_{0}
is the postcondition.
Upon entering the while loop, we encounter nested if statements, which we analyze with the
If
rule. This requires us to split
\varphi_{n+1}
into three components, satisfying
n<N\land y<M
,
n\geq N
, and
y\geq M
, respectively. The assertion
x\geq N
is only possible if we have already taken at least
N
steps, or in other words, if
n+1\leq(N+M)-N=M
. Letting
k
range from
\max(0,n+1-M)
to
0
therefore gives us a single term
k=0
when
n+1\leq M
and an empty conjunction otherwise. A similar argument holds when
y\geq M
. All the other outcomes go into the first branch, where we preclude the
k=0
and
k=n+1
cases since it must be true that
x\neq N
and
y\neq M
.

Let
P(n,k)=(x=N-k\land y=M-(n-k))
. Using this shorthand, the postcondition at the end of the if statement is obtained by taking an outcome conjunction of the results from the three branches.

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{k=\max(1,n+1-M)}^{\min(N,n)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P(n,k-1)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-(n+1)\choose N-k})}\oplus\bigoplus_{k=\max(1,n+1-M)}^{\min(N,n)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P(n,k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-(n+1)\choose N-k})}

\displaystyle\oplus\bigoplus_{k=\max(0,n+1-M)}^{0}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P(n,k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-(n+1)\choose N-k})}\oplus\bigoplus_{k=n+1}^{\min(N,n+1)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P(n,k-1)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-(n+1)\choose N-k})}

Now, we can combine the conjunctions with like terms.

\bigoplus_{k=\max(1,n+1-M)}^{\min(N,n+1)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P(n,k-1)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-(n+1)\choose N-k})}\oplus\bigoplus_{k=\max(0,n+1-M)}^{\min(N,n)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P(n,k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-(n+1)\choose N-k})}

And adjust the bounds on the first conjunction by subtracting 1 from the lower and upper bounds of
k
:

\bigoplus_{k=\max(0,n-M)}^{\min(N-1,n)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P(n,k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-(n+1)\choose N-(k+1)})}\oplus\bigoplus_{k=\max(0,n+1-M)}^{\min(N,n)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P(n,k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-(n+1)\choose N-k})}

Now, we examine when the bounds of these two conjunctions differ. If
n\geq M
, then the first conjunction has an extra
k=n-M
term. Similarly, the second conjunction has an extra
k=N
term when
n\geq N
.
Based on that observation, we split them as follows:

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{k\in\{n-M\mid n\geq M\}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P(n,k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-(n+1)\choose N-(k+1)})}\oplus\bigoplus_{k\in\{N\mid n\geq N\}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P(n,k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-(n+1)\choose N-k})}

\displaystyle\oplus\bigoplus_{k=\max(0,n+1-M)}^{\min(N-1,n)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P(n,k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-(n+1)\choose N-(k+1)}+{N+M-(n+1)\choose N-k})}

Knowing that
k=n-M
in the first conjunction, we get that:

{N+M-(n+1)\choose N-(k+1)}={N+M-(n+1)\choose N-(n-M+1)}=1={N+M-n\choose N-k}

Similarly, for the second conjunction we get the same weight. Also, observe that for any
a
and
b
:

\displaystyle{a\choose b}+{a\choose b+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{a!}{b!(a-b)!}+\frac{a!}{(b+1)!(a-b-1)!}

\displaystyle=\frac{a!}{b!(a-b)(a-b-1)!}+\frac{a!}{(b+1)b!(a-b-1)!}

\displaystyle=\frac{a!(b+1)+a!(a-b)}{(b+1)b!(a-b)(a-b-1)!}

\displaystyle=\frac{a!(b+1+a-b)}{(b+1)!(a-b)!}

\displaystyle=\frac{(a+1)!}{(b+1)!((a+1)-(b+1))!}

\displaystyle={a+1\choose b+1}

So, letting
a=N+M-(n+1)
and
b=N-(k+1)
, it follows that:

{N+M-(n+1)\choose N-(k+1)}+{N+M-(n+1)\choose N-k}={N+M-n\choose N-k}

We can therefore rewrite the assertion as follows:

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{k\in\{n-M\mid n\geq M\}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P(n,k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-n\choose N-k})}\oplus\bigoplus_{k\in\{N\mid n\geq N\}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}P(n,k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-n\choose N-k})}

\displaystyle\oplus\bigoplus_{k=\max(0,n+1-M)}^{\min(N-1,n)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P(n,k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-n\choose N-k})}

And by recombining the terms, we get:

\bigoplus_{k=\max(0,n-M)}^{\min(N,n)}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P(n,k)\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{({N+M-n\choose N-k})}

Which is precisely
\varphi_{n}
. According to the
Variant
rule, the final postcondition is just
\varphi_{0}
.

H.2.
Shortest Paths

Recall the following program that nondeterministically finds the shortest path from
s
to
t
using a model of computation based on the tropical semiring (
Example

3.11
).

\texttt{SP}\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{while}~pos\neq t~\texttt{do}\\
\quad next\coloneqq 1\fatsemi\\
\quad{\left(next\coloneqq next+1\right)}^{\langle next<N,~~G[pos][next]\rangle}\fatsemi\\
\quad pos\coloneqq next\fatsemi\\
\quad\texttt{assume}~1\end{array}\right.

The derivation is shown in
Figure

9
. We use the
While
rule to analyze the outer loop. This requires the following families of assertions, where
\varphi_{n}
represents the outcomes where the guard remains true after exactly
n
iterations and
\psi_{n}
represents the outcomes where the loop guard is false after
n
iterations. Let
I=\{1,\ldots,N\}\setminus\{t\}
.

\varphi_{n}\triangleq\bigoplus_{i\in I}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=i\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,i)+n)}

\psi_{n}\triangleq\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=t\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,t)+n)}\qquad\qquad\psi_{\infty}\triangleq\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}pos=t\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\mathsf{sp}(G,s,t))}

We now argue that
({\psi}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\rightsquigarrow{\psi}_{\infty}
. Take any
(m_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
m_{n}\vDash\psi_{n}
for each
n
, which means that
|m_{n}|=\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,t)+n
and
\mathsf{supp}(m_{n})\subseteq(pos=t)
. In the tropical semiring,
|m_{n}|
corresponds to the minimum weight of any element in
\mathsf{supp}(m_{n})
, so we know there is some
\sigma\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{n})
such that
m_{n}(\sigma)=\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,t)+n
, and since
\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,t)
is Boolean valued and
\mathsf{true}=0
and
\mathsf{false}=\infty
, then
m_{n}(\sigma)
is either
n
or
\infty
.

By definition, the minimum
n
for which
\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,t)=\mathsf{true}
is
\mathsf{sp}(G,s,t)
, so for all
n<\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,t)
, it must be the case that
|m_{n}|=\infty
and for all
n\geq\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,t)
, it must be the case that
|m_{n}|=n
. Now,
|\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}m_{n}|=\min_{n\in\mathbb{N}}|m_{n}|=\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,t)
, and since all elements of each
m_{n}
satisfies
pos=t
, then we get that
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}m_{n}\vDash\psi_{\infty}
.

Now, we will analyze the inner iteration using the
Iter
rule and the following two families of assertions, which we will assume are 1-indexed for simplicity of the proof.

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=s\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=i\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\mathsf{sp}_{0}^{t}(G,s,i))}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{0}\oplus\psi_{0}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\texttt{while}~pos\neq t~\texttt{do}\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{i\in I}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=i\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,i)+n)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\quad\;next\coloneqq 1\fatsemi\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}{\bigoplus_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=i\land next=1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,i)+n)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\quad\;{\left(next\coloneqq next+1\right)}^{\langle next<N,~~G[pos][next]\rangle}\fatsemi\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{j=1}^{N}{\bigoplus_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=i\land next=j\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{((\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,i)\land G[i][j])+n)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{j=1}^{N}{\bigoplus_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}next=j\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{((\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,i)\land G[i][j])+n)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\quad\;pos\coloneqq next\fatsemi\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{j=1}^{N}{\bigoplus_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=j\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{((\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,i)\land G[i][j])+n)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\quad\;\texttt{assume}~1\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{j=1}^{N}{\bigoplus_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=j\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{((\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,i)\land G[i][j])+n+1)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{j=1}^{N}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=j\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\mathsf{sp}_{n+1}^{t}(G,s,j)+n+1)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\varphi_{n+1}\oplus\psi_{n+1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\psi_{\infty}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=t\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\mathsf{sp}(G,s,t))}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\end{array}

Figure 9
.
Shortest path proof

\displaystyle\vartheta_{j}

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\bigoplus_{i\in I}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=i\land next=j\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,i)+n)}&\text{if}&j<N\\
\top\odot\dsser{0}&\text{if}&j\geq N\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\xi_{j}

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\bigoplus_{i\in I}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=i\land next=j\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{((\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,i)\land G[i][j])+n)}&\text{if}&j<N\\
\top\odot\dsser{0}&\text{if}&j\geq N\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\xi_{\infty}

\displaystyle\triangleq\bigoplus_{j=1}^{N}{\bigoplus_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=i\land next=j\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{((\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,i)\land G[i][j])+n)}

It is easy to see that
({\xi}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\rightsquigarrow{\xi}_{\infty}
since
\xi_{\infty}
is by definition an outcome conjunction of all the non-empty terms
\xi_{j}
. When
j<N
, then we get
\vartheta_{j}\vDash next<N
, so we dispatch the first proof obligation of the
Iter
rule as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\vartheta_{j}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{i\in I}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=i\land next=j\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,i)+n)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;\texttt{assume}~next<N\fatsemi\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{i\in I}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=i\land next=j\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,i)+n)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\\
\;next\coloneqq next+1\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\bigoplus_{i\in I}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=i\land next=j+1\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{(\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,i)+n)}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\implies\\
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\vartheta_{j+1}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}\end{array}

When instead
j\geq N
, then we know that
\vartheta_{j}\vDash\lnot(next<N)
and so it is easy to see that:

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\vartheta_{j}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{assume}~next<N\fatsemi next\coloneqq next+1~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\top\odot\dsser{0}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

For the second proof obligation, we must show that:

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\vartheta_{j}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}~\texttt{assume}~G[pos][next]~{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\langle}}\xi_{j}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{\rangle}}

For each outcome, we know that
pos=i
and
next=j
. If
G[i][j]=\mathsf{true}=0
, then
(\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,i)\land G[i][j])+n=\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,i)+n
, so the postcondition is unchanged. If
G[i][j]=\mathsf{false}=\infty
, then
(\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,i)\land G[i][j])+n=\infty
and the outcome is eliminated as expected.
We now justify the consequence after
\texttt{assume}~1
. Consider the term:

\bigoplus_{i\in I}\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\lceil\color[rgb]{0,0,0}pos=j\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\rceil^{((\mathsf{sp}_{n}^{t}(G,s,i)\land G[i][j])+n+1)}

This corresponds to just taking the outcome of minimum weight, which will be
n+1
if
\mathsf{sp}_{n}(G,s,i)\land G[i][j]
is true for some
i\in I
and
\infty
otherwise. By definition, this corresponds exactly to
\mathsf{sp}_{n+1}^{t}(G,s,j)+n+1
.
